Thus, AI is not perpendicular to plane BCD.\n   340\t\n   341\t(b) The orthogonal projection of A onto plane BCD coincides with the incenter of triangle BCD.\n   342\t\n   343\tIs it necessarily true? No.\n   344\t\n   345\tProof or counterexample: In a regular tetrahedron, the projection of A onto BCD is the centroid (which is also the incenter of equilateral BCD). However, consider a tangential tetrahedron where BCD is isosceles (e.g., BC = CD ≠ DB) and A is positioned such that the areas satisfy the proportionality and the tetrahedron is tangential with acute dihedral angles, but A is not directly above the incenter of BCD. For instance, adjust A so its projection is the centroid of BCD instead. The volume condition can still hold without requiring the projection to be the incenter. Thus, this is not necessarily true.\n   346\t\n   347\t(c) The distances from A to each of the three lines BC, CD, and DB are equal.\n   348\t\n   349\tIs it necessarily true? No.\n   350\t\n   351\tProof or counterexample: In a regular tetrahedron, these distances are equal due to symmetry. However, consider a tangential tetrahedron where BCD is scalene (e.g., BC ≠ CD ≠ DB), and A is positioned to satisfy the area proportionality and acute dihedral angles. The distances from A to the lines BC, CD, and DB depend on the specific geometry and are not required to be equal by the given conditions. For example, if BC is much longer than CD, the distance from A to BC could differ from that to CD while still satisfying the volume condition. Thus, this is not necessarily true.\n   352\t\n   353\t(d) The areas of triangles ABC, ACD, ADB are in the ratio BC: CD: DB.\n   354\t\n   355\tIs it necessarily true? Yes.\n   356\t\n   357\tProof: From the volume condition, as derived earlier:\n   358\t\n   359\tarea(ABC) / BC = area(ACD) / CD = area(ABD) / DB = m (some constant)\n   360\t\n   361\tTherefore, area(ABC) = m * BC, area(ACD) = m * CD, area(ABD) = m * DB\n   362\t\n   363\tThis implies area(ABC) : area(ACD) : area(ABD) = BC : CD : DB\n   364\t\n   365\tThus, the statement holds for every such tetrahedron.\n   366\t\n   367\t(e) The point at which the inscribed sphere touches face BCD is the incenter of triangle BCD.\n   368\t\n   369\tIs it necessarily true? Yes.\n   370\t\n   371\tProof: In a tangential tetrahedron, the inscribed sphere is tangent to each face at a point equidistant from the edges of that face. For a triangular face like BCD, the point equidistant from its three edges (BC, CD, DB) is the incenter of triangle BCD. Therefore, the point of tangency on face BCD is the incenter of BCD.\n   372\t\n   373\t(f) AB = AC = AD.\n   374\t\n   375\tIs it necessarily true? No.\n   376\t\n   377\tProof or counterexample: The conditions do not require the edges from A to be equal. For example, consider a tangential tetrahedron where BCD is equilateral, and A is positioned such that AB ≠ AC ≠ AD, but the areas still satisfy area(ABC) ∝ BC, etc., and the dihedral angles are acute. Adjusting the position of A while maintaining the tangential property and volume condition can result in unequal edges. Thus, this is not necessarily true.\n   378\t\n   379\t(g) Triangle ABC is isosceles with AB = AC.\n   380\t\n   381\tIs it necessarily true? No.\n   382\t\n   383\tProof or counterexample: The conditions do not specify the lengths AB and AC. For instance, in a tangential tetrahedron where BCD is scalene and A is positioned to satisfy the area proportionality, triangle ABC could be scalene (AB ≠ AC). As long as the volume condition and acute dihedral angles hold, AB and AC need not be equal. Thus, this is not necessarily true.\n   384\t\n   385\t(h) Triangle BCD is equilateral.\n   386\t\n   387\tIs it necessarily true?
